Table 1.
First page of the spectral sequence
E^{1}_{r,q}=H^{\textnormal{Alt}_{r+1}}_{q}\left(D^{r+1}\left(F\right),D^{r+1}\left(f_{t}\right)\right)
for a map germ
f:(X,S)\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{6},0)
in the pair of dimensions
(5,6)
(left) and the
input
objects required to compute it after the identifications
H_{m}^{\textnormal{Alt}}\big(D^{r+1}(F),D^{r+1}(f_{t})\big)\cong H_{m-1}^{\textnormal{Alt}}\big(D^{r+1}(f_{t})\big)
,
m>1
, and
H_{\bullet}^{\textnormal{Alt}}\big(D^{r+1}(F),D^{r+1}(f_{t})\big)=H_{\bullet}^{\textnormal{Alt}}\big(D^{r+1}(F)\big)
,
r+1>d(f)
(right).

The following lemma is stated for rational homology, the version with integer coefficients is explained in the remark that follows its proof.

Lemma 3.9

(cf.
[
14
, Theorem 4.6]
)
.

Let
f:(X,S)\to(\mathbb{C}^{p},0)
be an
\mathscr{A}
-finite map germ, where
(X,S)
is a germ of an
icis
of dimension
n<p
and codimension
r
. Consider a non-empty
D^{k}(f_{t})
and write
d
for
\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}D^{k}(f_{t})
. Then,

(1)

for
k\geq 2
,
H^{\textnormal{Alt}}_{q}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{k}(f_{t})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}=0
if
q\neq 0
or
q\notin\left[d+(1-r)k,d\right]
, and

(2)

H^{\textnormal{Alt}}_{q}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{1}(f_{t})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
is zero for
q\neq 0,d
.

Proof.

Observe that
D^{1}(f_{t})=X_{t}
is the Milnor fibre of the
icis

D^{1}(f)=X
, so we can assume that
k\geq 2
.

With rational coefficients, the homology of the alternating chains and the alternating part of the homology coincide (see
[
30
, Proposition 10.1]
), so we can use them indistinctly. In this proof we use the homology of the alternating chains to prove the statement.

For
q\geq d
the space
D^{k}(f_{t})
has the homotopy type of a
CW
-complex of dimension
d
, therefore, there are no alternating chains above
\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}D^{k}(f_{t})
. This proves the result for those
q
. It only remains to check when
q<\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}D^{k}(f_{t})
for
k\geq 2
. To prove the remaining part of the lemma, we take the proof of
[
14
, Theorem 4.6]
as reference. This argument consists of two steps: controlling the alternating homology of the pair
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{k}(F),D^{k}(f_{t})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
by virtue of
[
14
, Theorem 3.30]
, where
F
is a one parameter unfolding of
f
, and specify exactly when this pair can have alternating homology using
[
14
, Theorem 3.13]
.

The hypothesis of
[
14
, Theorem 3.13 and Theorem 3.30]
are not too restrictive, so we can take a one-parameter unfolding
F
of
f
and combine these theorems to prove that

H^{\textnormal{Alt}}_{q}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{k}(F),D^{k}(f_{t})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}=0

for

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle\leq\min\left\{(n+1-r+1)k-(p+1)(k-1)-1,nk-p(k-1)\right\}

\displaystyle=nk-p(k-1)+(1-r)k=d+(1-r)k.

Therefore, using the exact sequence of the pair and the fact that
D^{k}(F)
contracts to isolated points in an equivariant way, we have that
H^{\textnormal{Alt}}_{0}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{k}(f_{t})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}\cong H^{\textnormal{Alt}}_{0}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{k}(F)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
and
H^{\textnormal{Alt}}_{q-1}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{k}(f_{t})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}\cong H^{\textnormal{Alt}}_{q}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{k}(F),D^{k}(f_{t})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
, for
q>1
.
∎

Remark
.

As the reader should notice, this lemma can be stated for the homology of alternating chains (some times denoted by
AH
) instead of alternating homology and integer homology instead of rational homology. If one wants to use integer homology the same proof works changing
H^{\textnormal{Alt}}
for
AH
and adding in
Item

2
that the homology is free if
q=0,d
(which is trivial, because the group acting is
\Sigma_{1}
). Nevertheless,
Corollary

3.10
is well stated as it is, changing to rational coefficients to write
H^{\textnormal{Alt}}
.

Corollary 3.10
.

With the hypotheses of
Lemma

3.9
, the reduced integer homology of the image of a stable perturbation of
(X,f)
is zero except possibly in dimensions

(1)

p-k(p-n)+k-1+s
for all
0\leq s\leq(1-r)k
and
2\leq k\leq d(f)
,

(2)

d(f)-1
if
s(f)>d(f)
, and

(3)

n
if
X
is non-smooth (or
p=n+1
).

Proof.

The proof follows from the version of
Lemma

3.9
with integer coefficients and the homology of the alternating chains,
Theorem

3.7
and a careful inspection of the
icss
as in
Theorem

3.8
.
∎

Remark
.

Observe that, if
X
is a hypersurface, this theorem proves that the homology of the image appears in the same dimensions than the smooth case. Also, note that this theorem may not be sharp, because
[
14
, Theorem 3.13]
only gives a estimate to control the alternating homology of the pair
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{k}(F),D^{k}(f_{t})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
and it could be a bad estimate in general.

It is surprising that, in the case of corank one, the same proof does not prove
Theorem

3.8
. This makes us think that there is an argument that avoids the detail of the codimension of the
icis
:

Conjecture 3.11
.

Consider a map germ
f:(X,S)\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{p},0)
of finite
\mathscr{A}_{e}
-codimension, with
X

icis
of dimension
\operatorname{dim}X=n<p
. Then, the reduced integer homology of the image of a stable perturbation of
(X,f)
is zero except possibly in dimensions

(1)

p-k(p-n)k+k-1
for all
2\leq k\leq d(f)
,

(2)

d(f)-1
if
s(f)>d(f)
, and

(3)

n
if
X
is non-smooth.

Remark
.

This conjecture and
are related to
[
21
, Theorems 2.3 and 2.8]
, when the source is smooth and the map germ is not necessarily
\mathscr{A}
-finite but the dimensions of the multiple point spaces are controlled. They, and
Section

5
, are also closely related with
[
21
, Theorem 2.4]
in the particular case that
X
is the double point space
D^{2}(f)
.

Houston also uses
Theorem

3.7
in
[
17
]
with a versal unfolding
F
, of a multi-germ
f:(\mathbb{C}^{n},S)\to(\mathbb{C}^{n+1},0)
, and a section that gives the stable perturbation,
f_{t}
. Also, taking into account that the Euler-Poincaré characteristic of every page remains invariant, see
[
25
, Example 1.F]
, and that the image of the versal unfolding is contractible, it remains to compute
\chi\left(E^{1}_{*,*}\right)
to get
\mu_{I}(f)
, and the terms of the sum are arranged to define Houston’s alternating Milnor numbers,
\mu_{k}^{\textnormal{Alt}}(f)
(actually, he computes them through the limit of the spectral sequence, both ways give the same result).

These ideas and the previous proofs inspire the following definition (see also the simplification and developments of it in
[
8
]
, particularly how to determine
d(f)
when
s(f)>d(f)
with
[
8
, Lemma 3.3]
).

Definition 3.12
.

Given an
\mathscr{A}
-finite map germ
f:(X,S)\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{n+1},0)
of corank one, with
X

icis
of dimension
n
, the
k
-th alternating Milnor number of
(X,f)
, denoted as
\mu_{k}^{\textnormal{Alt}}(X,f)
, is defined by

\mu_{k}^{\textnormal{Alt}}(X,f)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\textnormal{rank }H^{\textnormal{Alt}_{k}}_{n-k+2}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{k}(F),D^{k}(f_{t});\mathbb{Z}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}},\quad\quad\text{if $1\leq k\leq d(f)$}\\
\\
\displaystyle{s(f)-1\choose d(f)},\quad\quad\text{ if $k=d(f)+1$ and $s(f)>d(f)$}\\
\\
0,\quad\quad\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

being
F
its versal unfolding and
f_{t}
a stable perturbation.

These numbers are very useful because they decompose the image Milnor number. This was used by Houston in
[
17
, Definition 3.11]
for the smooth case of corank one, we extend it to the non-smooth case of corank one.

Proposition 3.13
.

For
f:(X,S)\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{n+1},0)

\mathscr{A}
-finite of corank one and
X
an
icis
of dimension
n
,

\sum_{k}\mu_{k}^{\textnormal{Alt}}\left(X,f\right)=\mu_{I}\left(X,f\right).

Proof.

From the proof of
Theorem

3.8
, we only have to check that
\mu^{\textnormal{Alt}}_{d(f)+1}(X,f)
coincides with the (rank of the) remaining non-zero entries of the spectral sequence after collapsing, i.e., we have to check that

\operatorname{rank}E^{2}_{d(f),0}={s\left(f\right)-1\choose d\left(f\right)}.

From
[
17
, Lemma 3.3]
, which can be stated for general stable map germs with a verbatim proof, or the constancy of the Euler-Poincaré characteristic of the spectral sequence (see
[
25
, Example 1.F]
) we have

\operatorname{rank}E^{2}_{d(f),0}=\left|\sum_{\ell=d\left(f\right)+1}^{s\left(f\right)}\left(-1\right)^{\ell}{s\left(f\right)\choose\ell}\right|={s\left(f\right)-1\choose d\left(f\right)}.\qed

One term deserves a bit of attention:
\mu_{1}(X,f)
. This term does not appear in the smooth case because it is zero but, in general, it is equal to
\mu(X)
(see
Figure

2
).
Proposition

3.13
allows us to reduce the weak form of Mond’s conjecture for
icis
to the smooth case.

Figure 2.
The first alternating Milnor number and its relationship with the deformations of
(X,f)
and the
\mu_{I}(X,f)
. Here, one can also appreciate the conclusions of
Theorems

2.14
and
2.15
.

Corollary 3.14

(cf.
[
8
, Theorem 3.9]
)
.

For
f:(X,S)\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{n+1},0)

\mathscr{A}
-finite of corank one and
X
an
icis
of dimension
n
,
\mu_{I}(X,f)=0
if, and only if,
(X,f)
is stable.

Proof.

One direction is trivial.

If
\mu_{I}(X,f)=0
then
\mu_{1}^{\textnormal{Alt}}(X,f)=\mu(X)=0
and we are in the case of smooth domain, proved in
[
8
, Theorem 3.9]
.
∎

Remark
.

Note that the weak form of Mond’s conjecture for the smooth case in any corank implies, with the same proof of
Corollary

3.14
, the same conjecture for
icis
in any corank by means of
Proposition

3.13
, which can be stated for any corank using
Lemma

3.9
and always carries a term equal to
\mu(X)
.

4.
A Lê-Greuel type formula

Now that we have a basic building of the image Milnor number with
icis
in the source, our last preparatory step is to prove a Lê-Greuel type formula for
\mu_{I}(X,f)
. In
[
32
]
I. Pallarés-Torres and the second named author proved a Lê-Greuel type formula in the setting of the image Milnor number in the smooth case and finitely determined map germs. Recall the original Lê-Greuel formula, see
[
12
,
39
]
,

\mu(X,0)+\mu(X\cap H,0)=\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{\mathcal{O}_{n}}{(g)+J(g,p)}.

Here,
(X,0)
is an
icis
with defining equation
g
,
p
a function such that
(X\cap H,0)
is an
icis
as well, where
H\coloneqq p^{-1}(0)
, and
J(g,p)
is the ideal generated by the minors of maximum order of the Jacobian matrix of
(g,p)
. Taking into account that the right hand side of the equation could be seen as the number of critical points of
p
restricted to the Milnor fiber of
X
, it is obvious that the main theorem of
[
32
]
is a similar result for the context of map germs when
p
is a generic linear projection (see
Figure

3
):

Theorem 4.1

(see
[
32
, Theorem 3.2]
)
.

Let
f:(\mathbb{C}^{n},0)\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{n+1},0)
be a corank 1 and
\mathscr{A}
-finite map germ with
n>1
. Let
p:\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a generic linear projection which defines a transverse slice
g:(\mathbb{C}^{n-1},0)\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{n},0)
. Then

\textnormal{{\#}}\Sigma\left(\left.p\right|_{Z_{s}}\right)=\mu_{I}\left(f\right)+\mu_{I}\left(g\right),

where
\textnormal{{\#}}\Sigma\left(\left.p\right|_{Z_{s}}\right)
is the number of critical points on all the strata of
Z_{s}\coloneqq\text{Im}\left(f_{s}\right)
, being
f_{s}
a stable perturbation of
f
.

If
p\colon\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is a generic linear projection, then the
transverse slice
is, by definition, the restriction
f\colon\big(f^{-1}(H),0\big)\to(H,0)
, where
H
is the hyperplane
H=p^{-1}(0)
. When
f
has corank one, we can choose coordinates in such a way that the transverse slice is in fact a mapping
g:(\mathbb{C}^{n-1},0)\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{n},0)
and
f
is an unfolding of
g
(see
[
23
, pp. 1380–1381]
).

Figure 3.
Depiction of the Lê-Greuel type formula for map germs.

The stratification considered in the image of the stable perturbation
f_{s}
in the theorem above is the
stratification by stable types
(see
[
30
, Definition 7.2]
). We recall that two points
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
in the image of
f_{s}
belong to the same stratum if, and only if, the multi-germs of
f_{s}
at such points are
\mathscr{A}
-equivalent. Since
f_{s}
has only corank one singularities, each stratum is determined by a partition
\gamma(k)
, with
1\leq k\leq n+1
and it is, in fact, a Whitney stratification (see
[
30
, Corollary 7.5]
).

We prove a similar result for an
icis
in the source and multi-germs. The first step is to work with Marar’s formula for our setting of multi-germs and
icis
in the source. Fortunately, his proof is essentially combinatorial and one can prove the version we need with almost no modifications. Hence, if
\gamma(k)=(r_{1},\dots,r_{m})
is a partition of
k
and
\alpha_{i}=\texttt{\#}\left\{j:r_{j}=i\right\}
, Marar’s formula is the following.

Theorem 4.2

(cf.
[
22
, Theorem 3.1]
)
.

Let
f_{0}:(\mathbb{C}^{n},0)\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{p},0)
of corank 1 and
\mathscr{A}
-finite,
2\leq n<p
, and consider its stable perturbation
f_{t}:X_{t}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{p}
. Then

\chi\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}f_{t}(X_{t})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}=a_{0}\chi\left(U_{t}\right)+\sum_{k\geq 2}\sum_{\gamma(k)}a_{\gamma(k)}\chi\left(D^{k}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}f_{t},\gamma(k)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}\right),

where
a_{0}=1
and

a_{\gamma(k)}=\frac{(-1)^{\sum\alpha_{i}+1}}{\prod_{i\geq 1}i^{\alpha_{i}}\alpha_{i}!}

if
D^{k}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}f_{t},\gamma(k)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
is non-empty, and zero otherwise.

Now, by
Theorem

4.2
but stated for a stable perturbation of an
\mathscr{A}
-finite corank
1
map germ
f:(X,S)\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{n+1},0)
, with
\operatorname{dim}X=n
, we have

(2)

\displaystyle 1+(-1)^{n}

\displaystyle\mu_{I}(X,f)=\texttt{\#}S+(-1)^{n}\mu(X)

\displaystyle+\sum_{k\geq 2}\sum_{\gamma(k)}a_{\gamma(k)}\left(\beta_{0}^{\gamma(k)}+(-1)^{\operatorname{dim}D^{k}\left(f,\gamma(k)\right)}\mu\left(D^{k}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}f,\gamma(k)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}\right)\right),

where
\texttt{\#}S
is the number of points in
S
and
\beta_{0}^{\gamma(k)}
the zero Betti number of
D^{k}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}f,\gamma(k)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
.

With
Theorem

4.1
in mind, we take a generic linear projection
p:\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, with kernel
H\cong\mathbb{C}^{n}
, which defines a transverse slice
g\coloneqq\left.f\right|:\big(X\cap f^{-1}(H),S\big)\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{n},0)
.

Observe that
X\cap f^{-1}(H)
, which we will call
\tilde{X}
to simplify notation, is still an
icis
. Following the steps of
[
32
]
from here, notice that, if
\operatorname{dim}D^{k}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}f,\gamma(k)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}>0
, then
\operatorname{dim}D^{k}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}f,\gamma(k)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}-1=\operatorname{dim}D^{k}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}g,\gamma(k)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
and, if
\operatorname{dim}D^{k}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}f,\gamma(k)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}=0
, then the fiber of
D^{k}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}g,\gamma(k)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
(i.e.,
D^{k}\big(g_{s},\gamma(k)\big)
for a stable perturbation
g_{s}
) is empty, by
Lemma

3.5
. Therefore, if we apply the previous formula to
(\tilde{X},g)
, we get

(3)

\displaystyle 1+(-1

\displaystyle)^{n-1}\mu_{I}(\tilde{X},g)=\texttt{\#}S+(-1)^{n-1}\mu(\tilde{X})

\displaystyle+\,\,\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq 2,\,\gamma(k):\\
\operatorname{dim}D^{k}(f,\gamma(k))>0\end{subarray}}}\ \ \ a_{\gamma(k)}\left(\beta_{0}^{\gamma(k)}+(-1)^{\operatorname{dim}D^{k}\left(f,\gamma(k)\right)-1}\mu\left(D^{k}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}g,\gamma(k)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}\right)\right),

If we subtract
Equation

3
from
Equation

2
, and use that
\operatorname{dim}D^{k}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}f,\gamma(k)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}=n+1-k-k+\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}
(see
Lemma

3.5
), we have

\displaystyle\mu_{I}(X,f)+\mu_{I}

\displaystyle(g,\tilde{X})=\mu(X)+\mu(\tilde{X})

\displaystyle+\,\,\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq 2,\,\gamma(k):\\
\operatorname{dim}D^{k}(f,\gamma(k))=0\end{subarray}}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\frac{(-1)^{\sum\alpha_{i}+1+n}}{\prod_{i\geq 1}i^{\alpha_{i}}\alpha_{i}!}\left(\beta_{0}^{\gamma(k)}+\mu\left(D^{k}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}f,\gamma(k)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\,\,\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq 2,\,\gamma(k):\\
\operatorname{dim}D^{k}(f,\gamma(k))>0\end{subarray}}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\frac{1}{\prod_{i\geq 1}i^{\alpha_{i}}\alpha_{i}!}\left(\mu\left(D^{k}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}f,\gamma(k)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}\right)+\mu\left(D^{k}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}g,\gamma(k)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}\right)\right),

where we have simplified the signs expanding
a_{\gamma(k)}
, and
\beta_{0}^{\gamma(k)}
denotes the same as before.

Once we arrive here, we can keep simplifying signs: if
\operatorname{dim}D^{k}(f,\gamma(k))=\sum\alpha_{i}+1+n-2k=0
, then the first sign is positive.

On the other hand, we can choose a generic projection and coordinates on source and target so that
p(y_{1},\dots,y_{n+1})=y_{1}
. Moreover,

D^{k}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}g,\gamma(k)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}=D^{k}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}f,\gamma(k)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}\cap\tilde{p}^{-1}(0),

where
\tilde{p}:X\times\mathbb{C}^{k-1}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is the projection on the first coordinate for every
k
, seeing
D^{k}(f)
as a subset of
X\times\mathbb{C}^{k-1}
(recall the comments below
Example

3.3
), and it is generic as well (in general it would be a mapping induced by
p\circ f
).

By the comments above, the structure of
icis
given in
Lemma

3.5
and the Lê-Greuel-type formula for
icis
; we have

\mu\left(D^{k}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}f,\gamma(k)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}\right)+\mu\left(D^{k}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}g,\gamma(k)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}\right)=\texttt{\#}\Sigma\left(\left.\tilde{p}\right|_{D^{k}(f_{s},\gamma(k))}\right)

and

\mu(X)+\mu(\tilde{X})=\texttt{\#}\Sigma\left(\left.\tilde{p}\right|_{X_{s}}\right),

where
f_{s}
and
X_{s}
are the stable perturbations of
f
and
X
.

Moreover, note that, if
\operatorname{dim}D^{k}(f,\gamma(k))=0
, then

\mu\left(D^{k}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}f,\gamma(k)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}\right)=m_{0}\left(D^{k}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}f,\gamma(k)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}\right)-\beta_{0}^{\gamma(k)},

where
m_{0}\left(D^{k}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}f,\gamma(k)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}\right)
is the multiplicity of
D^{k}\big(f,\gamma(k)\big)
. This can also be seen as the number of critical points of
\left.\tilde{p}\right|_{D^{k}(f_{s},\gamma(k))}
.

In conclusion,

\mu_{I}(X,f)+\mu_{I}(g,\tilde{X})=\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{\gamma(k)}\frac{\texttt{\#}\Sigma\left(\left.\tilde{p}\right|_{D^{k}(f_{s},\gamma(k))}\right)}{\prod_{i\geq 1}i^{\alpha_{i}}\alpha_{i}!}.

This is exactly the same point Pallarés-Torres and the second author, Nuño-Ballesteros, reach in
[
32
, Theorem 3.2]
. The theorem below follows from there (see
Figure

3
).

Theorem 4.3

(see
[
32
, Theorem 3.2]
)
.

For an
\mathscr{A}
-finite map germ
f:\left(X,S\right)\rightarrow\left(\mathbb{C}^{n+1},0\right)
of corank
1
from an
icis

X
of dimension
\operatorname{dim}X=n\geq 2
, let
p:\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a generic linear projection which defines a transverse slice
g:\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}X\cap\left(p\circ f\right)^{-1}\left(0\right),S\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}\rightarrow\left(\mathbb{C}^{n},0\right)
. Then,

\mu_{I}\left(f,X\right)+\mu_{I}\left(g,X\cap\left(p\circ f\right)^{-1}\left(0\right)\right)=\textnormal{{\#}}\Sigma\left(\left.p\right|_{Z_{s}}\right),

where
\textnormal{{\#}}\Sigma\left(\left.p\right|_{Z_{s}}\right)
is the number of critical points in the stratified sense of
p
restricted to
Z_{s}\coloneqq\text{Im}\left(f_{s}\right)
, being
f_{s}
a stable perturbation of
f
.

We complete
Theorem

4.3
with the case of a one-dimensional
icis

X
.

Proposition 4.4

(see
[
32
, Theorem 3.1]
)
.

Let
f:(X,S)\to(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)
be an injective map germ from an
icis

X
of dimension one. Consider a generic linear projection
p:\mathbb{C}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}
, then

\mu_{I}(X,f)+m_{0}(f)-1=\textnormal{{\#}}\Sigma\left(\left.p\right|_{Z_{s}}\right),

where
\textnormal{{\#}}\Sigma\left(\left.p\right|_{Z_{s}}\right)
is the number of critical points in the stratified sense of
p
restricted to
Z_{s}\coloneqq\text{Im}\left(f_{s}\right)
, being
f_{s}
a stable perturbation of
f
, and
m_{0}(f)=\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{O}_{X,S}/f^{*}\mathfrak{m}_{2}
the multiplicity of
f
.

Proof.

We have two strata in the image
Z_{s}
of the stable perturbation
(X_{s},f_{s})
: the 0-dimensional stratum
Z_{s}^{0}
given by the transverse double points and the 1-dimensional stratum
Z_{s}^{1}=f_{s}(X_{s})\setminus Z_{s}^{0}
.

Obviously,
\textnormal{{\#}}\Sigma\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}\left.p\right|_{Z_{s}^{0}}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
is equal to
\textnormal{{\#}}Z_{s}^{0}
, which is equal to
\mu_{2}^{\textnormal{Alt}}(X,f)
. Since
f_{s}
is a local diffeomorphism on
Z_{s}^{1}
,
\textnormal{{\#}}\Sigma\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}\left.p\right|_{Z_{s}^{1}}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
is equal to the number of critical points of
p\circ f_{s}
on
X_{s}
(here the points of
Z_{s}^{0}
can be excluded by genericity of
p
). By the usual Lê-Greuel formula for
X
and
X\cap(p\circ f)^{-1}(0)
, we have

\textnormal{{\#}}\Sigma\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}p\circ f_{s}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}=\mu(X)+\deg(p\circ f)-1.

But, again, the genericity of
p
implies that
\deg(p\circ f)=m_{0}(f)
. Hence,

\displaystyle\textnormal{{\#}}\Sigma\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}\left.p\right|_{Z_{s}^{0}}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}+\textnormal{{\#}}\Sigma\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}\left.p\right|_{Z_{s}^{1}}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}

\displaystyle=\mu_{2}^{\textnormal{Alt}}(X,f)+\mu(X)+m_{0}(f)-1

\displaystyle=\mu_{I}(X,f)+m_{0}(f)-1,

by
Proposition

3.13
.
∎

5.
The double point Milnor number

Finding conditions for a 1-parameter family to be Whitney equisingular requires working on the source and in the target separately. In the case of the source, we need to assure some structure and, as the reader could guess, the double point set is the best candidate. Furthermore, this set is the projection of an
icis
, the double point space, if the map is nice enough (see
Figure

4
). We have some invariants in this sense.

Definition 5.1
.

The double point set of

f:(\mathbb{C}^{n},S)\to(\mathbb{C}^{n+1},0)
, of finite singularity type, is the projection on the first coordinate of
D^{2}(f)
, and we denote it by
D(f)
. Furthermore, if
f
is
\mathscr{A}
-finite, we will define the
double point Milnor number
as

\mu_{D}(f)\coloneqq\beta_{n-1}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D(f_{t})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}},

where
f_{t}
is a stable perturbation of
f
.

Remark
.

The double point Milnor number was denoted as
\mu_{\Sigma_{2}}
in
[
15
]
by Houston.

Note that we have to define
\mu_{D}(f)
through a stable perturbation of
f
because
D(f)
is a hypersurface with possibly non-isolated singularities, hence may not have finite Milnor number in the ordinary sense of a hypersurface. However, we can still use
Theorem

2.11
to prove that
D(f_{t})
in
Definition

5.1
has the homotopy type of a wedge of spheres of middle dimension, as a small deformation of
D(f)
is topologically trivial in a Milnor sphere (see
Definition

2.10
).

The main reason to use this invariant is that
\mu_{D}(f)
is the image Milnor number of certain
(X,f)
if
f
has corank one, in that case
\mu_{D}(f)
coincides with
\mu_{I}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}(f),\pi\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
. Hence, we can use all the machinery we developed above if
f
has corank one. Firstly,
\mu_{D}(f)
is well defined by
Proposition

2.12
. Secondly, there are triple points of
f_{t}
that also correspond to double points of
\pi
and give rise to more homology (as
Figure

4
represents, there we have depicted a vague idea of the generators of the homology because some higher-dimensional properties cannot be made visible). Finally, the invariant
\mu_{D}(f)
is also conservative by
Theorem

2.14
:

Corollary 5.2
.

Let
f:(\mathbb{C}^{n},S)\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{n+1},0)
be finitely
\mathscr{A}
-determined, of corank
1
, and
f_{u}
a one-parameter perturbation of
f=f_{0}
. Take a representative of the unfolding such that its codomain,
B_{\epsilon}
, is a Milnor ball. Then,

\mu_{D}(f)=\beta_{n-1}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D(f_{u})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}+\sum_{y\in B_{\epsilon}}\mu_{D}(f_{u},y).

Remark
.

Observe that
Definition

5.1
can be generalized for every pair of dimensions
(n,p)
as long as
n<p
and
f
has corank one. Despite the fact that
D(f)
could not be a hypersurface,
D^{2}(f)
is an
icis
in this case (see
Lemma

3.2
) and we can define
\mu_{D}(f)
by means of
Theorem

3.8
(one can also use the Euler-Poincaré characteristic of the image of a stable perturbation, see for example
[
31
]
).

Figure 4.
Representation of how the homology of the double point set of a stable perturbation works.

Once more, we focus on the multiple point spaces but, in this case, we deal with
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}(f),\pi\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
and
D^{k}(\pi)
, where
f
has corank one and it is
\mathscr{A}
-finite. Using the
principle of iteration
(cf.
[
20
, 4.1]
), the multiple point spaces of a perturbation of
f
are isomorphic to the multiple point spaces of a perturbation of
\pi
with a shift in the multiplicity, and the same is true for unfoldings
F
and
\Pi
(of
f
and
\pi
, respectively). More precisely,
D^{k}(\pi_{t})\cong D^{k+1}(f_{t})
and
D^{k}(\Pi)\cong D^{k+1}(F)
, where the first isomorphism is given by

(4)

\displaystyle\phi\colon D^{k}(\pi_{t})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow D^{k+1}(f_{t})

\displaystyle\big((x,x_{1}),\dots,(x,x_{k})\big)

\displaystyle\longmapsto(x,x_{1},\dots,x_{k}),

and the second one is analogous. This inspires us to compare
\mu_{k}^{\textnormal{Alt}}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}(f),\pi\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
and
\mu_{k+1}^{\textnormal{Alt}}(f)
, which determine
\mu_{D}(f)
and
\mu_{I}(f)
, respectively. The relation is straightforward considering that

\displaystyle\mu_{k}^{\textnormal{Alt}}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}(f),\pi\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}

\displaystyle=\textnormal{rank }H^{\textnormal{Alt}_{k}}_{n-1-k+2}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{k}(\Pi),D^{k}(\pi_{t})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}

\displaystyle=\textnormal{rank }H^{\textnormal{Alt}_{k}}_{n-1-k+2}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{k+1}(F),D^{k+1}(f_{t})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}

and

\mu_{k+1}^{\textnormal{Alt}}(f)=\textnormal{rank }H^{\textnormal{Alt}_{k+1}}_{n-1-k+2}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{k+1}(F),D^{k+1}(f_{t})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}.

More precisely, the difference between
\mu^{\textnormal{Alt}}_{k}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}(f),\pi\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
and
\mu_{k+1}^{\textnormal{Alt}}(f)
is the group of permutations that acts.

To ease the notation, we write
H
instead of
H_{n-1-k+2}\big(D^{k+1}(F),D^{k+1}(f_{t})\big)
and
k
will be clear from the context. Consequently, we want to compare the alternating actions of
\Sigma_{k+1}
and
\Sigma_{k}<\Sigma_{k+1}
on
H
, where
\Sigma_{k}
acts as a subgroup fixing the first entry (by construction of the isomorphism of
Equation

4
). We will use representation theory to do this. For this reason, we will see
H
as a
\mathbb{C}
-vector space.

Recall that for each partition of
N
, say
\gamma(N)
, there is associated an irreducible representation of
\Sigma_{N}
(see
[
36
, Proposition 1.10.1]
). We will call this representation the
\gamma(N)
-representation
. The representation that acts by its sign is associated to the partition
(1,\dots,1)
, and we call it the
alternating representation
. Moreover, from the branching rules (see
[
36
, Theorem 2.8.3]
), we know that the alternating representation of
\Sigma_{N}
appears as a restriction of
\Sigma_{N+1}
from both the alternating representation and the
(2,1,\dots,1)
-representation. Therefore, knowing the character of the last one will be useful. Unfortunately, we could not find it in the literature.

Lemma 5.3
.

The character of the irreducible representation associated to the partition
(2,1,\dots,1)
is

sign(\sigma)\left(\textnormal{fix}\left(\sigma\right)-1\right),

where
\textnormal{fix}(\sigma)
is the number of entries fixed by the permutation
\sigma
.

Proof.

If
N=3
, the result is trivial. Assume that
N>3
; then, by
[
4
, Exercise 4.14]
, we know that the representation associated to this partition is either the standard or the tensor product of the standard and the alternating representations. By a careful inspection (but see also
[
4
, Exercise 4.6]
), we know that it is not the standard representation, therefore its character is the product of the standard and the alternating representations.

∎

For any representation
V
of a finite group
G
there is a decomposition into irreducible representations where there could be multiple copies of the same irreducible representation, see for example
[
4
, Proposition 1.8]
. For any partition
\gamma(N)
of
N
, the
\gamma(N)
-isotype
, also written as
V^{\gamma(N)}
, is the sum of those copies of the
\gamma(N)
-representation. In the particular case of the
(1,\dots,1)
-isotype we will use the name
alternating isotype
, as well. Hence, a useful object is the projection onto an isotype.

The projection onto the
(2,1,\dots,1)
-isotype can be made explicit considering we know the character and the dimension of the irreducible representation associated to this partition (see
[
4
, Section 2.4]
):

P_{k+1}\coloneqq\frac{k}{(k+1)!}\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{k+1}}sign(\sigma)\left(\textnormal{fix}(\sigma)-1\right)\sigma.

Remark
.

Observe that one can define the projection
P_{k+1}
with domain any space where
\Sigma_{k+1}
acts, here we will define it on
H
if nothing is said.

Theorem 5.4
.

Let
f:(\mathbb{C}^{n},S)\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{n+1},0)
be
\mathscr{A}
-finite of corank
1
. Then,

\mu_{k+1}^{\textnormal{Alt}}(f)\leq\mu_{k}^{\textnormal{Alt}}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}(f),\pi\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}

for
k=1,\dots,n
. Furthermore,

(1)

for
k=2,\dots,n
,
\mu_{k+1}^{\textnormal{Alt}}(f)=\mu_{k}^{\textnormal{Alt}}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}(f),\pi\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
if, and only if,
P_{k+1}\equiv 0
(or, equivalently, the
(2,1,\dots,1)
-isotype is zero), and

(2)

for
k=1
,
\mu_{2}^{\textnormal{Alt}}(f)=\mu\left(D^{2}(f)\right)
if, and only if, the space
H_{n-1}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}(f_{t})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
coincides with its alternating isotype, for
f_{t}
a stable perturbation of
f
.

Proof.

For
k=2,\dots,n
, from the branching rules, we know that the alternating isotype of the
\Sigma_{k}
representation on
H
comes exactly from the alternating isotype and the
(2,1,\dots,1)
-isotype of the representation of
\Sigma_{k+1}
.

Moreover, the former isotype contributes with the same dimension it has but the latter makes a contribution of one dimension for each
k
-dimensional copy it has. This comes from the fact that, in this isotype, every copy of the
(2,1,\dots,1)
-representation has dimension
k
and each one splits into an alternating representation of dimension
1
and an irreducible
(2,1,\dots,1)
-representation of dimension
k-1
when we restrict it to the subgroup
\Sigma_{k}
.

As the only difference between
\mu_{k+1}^{\textnormal{Alt}}(f)
and
\mu_{k}^{\textnormal{Alt}}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}(f),\pi\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
is the different groups acting,
\Sigma_{k+1}
and
\Sigma_{k}
as a subgroup, the result follows for these cases.

On the other hand,
P_{k+1}\equiv 0
if, and only if, there is no
(2,1,\dots,1)
-isotype, so
Item

1
follows.

Finally,
Item

2
is trivial, as the only two possible representations of
\Sigma_{2}
are the trivial and the alternating one.
∎

Remark
.

It is possible to determine the difference between
\mu_{k+1}^{\textnormal{Alt}}(f)
and
\mu_{k}^{\textnormal{Alt}}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}(f),\pi\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
, for it depends on the number of repetitions of the
(2,1,\dots,1)
-representation. For example, one can compute it through the inner product between the character of the whole representation of
\Sigma_{k+1}
and the
(2,1,\dots,1)
-representation (see
[
4
, Corollary 2.16]
), which is nothing more than counting the number of generators fixed by
P_{k}
in a convenient basis.

Also, for
k=n
and
k=n+1
, one is dealing with zero homology and the multiple point spaces are points, this ease the relation and we can say something more.

Theorem 5.5
.

With the hypotheses of
Theorem

5.4
, for
k=n
,

(n+1)\textnormal{ rank }H_{0}^{\textnormal{Alt}_{n+1}}\left(D^{n+1}(f_{t})\right)=\textnormal{rank }H_{0}^{\textnormal{Alt}_{n}}\left(D^{n+1}(f_{t})\right).

Also, for
k=n+1
,

\frac{d(f)s(f)^{2}}{s(f)-1}\mu_{n+2}^{\textnormal{Alt}}(f)=\mu_{n+1}^{\textnormal{Alt}}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}(f),\pi\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}.

Proof.

Now, we are dealing with points and the zero homology. In particular, we can identify the elements in the homology with the 0-chains. Hence, one can find a basis of
H_{0}^{\textnormal{Alt}_{n+1}}\left(D^{n+1}(f_{t})\right)
with elements given using the orbits of points
p
, for
p\in D^{n+1}(f_{t})
:

\sum_{\sigma}sign(\sigma)\sigma(p).

The action of
\Sigma_{n+1}
on the homology of the orbit of
p
is the regular representation, so it decomposes into the alternating representation we are considering,
n

(2,1,\dots,1)
-subrepresentations and more irreducible subrepresentations (see
[
4
, p. 17]
). The contributions to the alternating isotype of the representation of
\Sigma_{n}
come from: one alternating representation from each
(2,1,\dots,1)
-subrepresentation, each one of the alternating subrepresentation of
\Sigma_{n+1}
will be preserved in the subgroup, and there are no more contributions from other isotypes. This happens for every orbit of points in
D^{n+1}(f_{t})
, proving the first statement.

To prove the second part, recall that
\mu_{n+2}^{\textnormal{Alt}}(f)
comes from the bottom row of the spectral sequence (see, for example,
Table

1
), and the argument is similar but, now, working with the multiple point space of the unfolding. Therefore, again, the alternating isotype of
\Sigma_{k}
is
k+1
times bigger than the alternating isotype of
\Sigma_{k+1}
. Hence, if originally
\mu_{n+2}^{\textnormal{Alt}}(f)
was

\left|\sum_{\ell=d(f)+1}^{s(f)}(-1)^{l}{s(f)\choose\ell}\right|={s(f)-1\choose d(f)},

now,
\mu_{n+1}^{\textnormal{Alt}}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}(f),\pi\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
is

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{\ell=d(f)+1}^{s(f)}(-1)^{\ell}\ell{s(f)\choose\ell}\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{d(f)\left(d(f)+1\right)}{s(f)-1}{s(f)\choose d(f)+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{d(f)s(f)^{2}}{s(f)-1}{s(f)-1\choose d(f)}.\qed

Remark
.

Although these inequalities and equalities are enough for our purposes, one can specify the relation of
\mu_{n+1}^{\textnormal{Alt}}(f)
and
\mu_{n}^{\textnormal{Alt}}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}(f),\pi\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
using the ideas of the second part of the proof of
Theorem

5.5
and considering the exact sequence of the pair.

Also, one may ask what happens if the group acts by permutation of the elements of a base for some
k<n
(this action could be not faithful). Regarding this, there are algorithms to compute the alternating part based in the same idea: looking for orbits and the relation between the actions. An upper bound is also possible with the same ideas.

There are some interesting corollaries of
Theorems

5.4
and
5.5
. For example, it could happen that there is not enough space in the homology group to fit a
(2,1,\dots,1)
-subrepresentation.

Corollary 5.6
.

With the notation of
Theorem

5.4
, for
k=1,\dots,n
, if

\operatorname{rank}\left(H_{n-1-k+2}\big(D^{k+1}(F),D^{k+1}(f_{t})\big)\right)-\mu_{k+1}^{\textnormal{Alt}}(f)<k,

then

\mu_{k+1}^{\textnormal{Alt}}(f)=\mu_{k}^{\textnormal{Alt}}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}(f),\pi\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}.

Proof.

The proof is based on the fact that a
(2,1,\dots,1)
-representation of
\Sigma_{k}
has dimension
k
and the ideas of
Theorems

5.4
and
5.5
.
∎

Another example is an inequality involving the full Milnor number on both contexts.

Corollary 5.7
.

For
f
as in
Theorem

5.4
,
\mu_{I}(f)\leq\mu_{D}(f)
. This holds with equality if, and only if,
H_{n-1}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}(f_{t})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
coincides with its alternating isotype and all the
P_{i}
are zero for all
i
, for
P_{i}
as in
Theorem

5.4
.

Thence, there are some nice characterizations as well, in particular weak Mond’s conjecture for
\mu_{D}(f)
.

Corollary 5.8

(see
[
8
, Theorem 3.9]
)
.

For
f
as in
Theorem

5.4
, the following are equivalent:

(1)

f
is stable,

(2)

\mu_{I}(f)=0
, and

(3)

\mu_{D}(f)=0
.

Proof.

If
f
is stable, then
\mu_{D}(f)=\mu_{I}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}(f),\pi\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}=0
as so are all the alternating Milnor numbers. If
0=\mu_{D}(f)
, then
\mu_{I}(f)=0
by
Corollary

5.7
, but, if
\mu_{I}(f)=0
, then
f
is stable by the weak Mond’s conjecture for corank
1
(see
[
8
, Theorem 3.9]
).
∎

For an
f:(\mathbb{C}^{n},S)\to(\mathbb{C}^{n+1},0)
of corank one, we have studied the homology of the projection of
D^{2}(f_{t})
onto
\mathbb{C}^{n}
and we have compared it with the homology of the image of
f_{t}
, for
f_{t}
a stable perturbation of
f
. One can keep looking for relations between the multiple point spaces using the same ideas.

On one hand we can reproduce the roles of the image of
f_{t}
and
D(f_{t})
easily:
