\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

where
\pi_{k-1}^{k}:D^{k}(\bullet)\to D^{k-1}(\bullet)
is the projection that forgets the last entry, for
k=2,\dots,d(f)
, and
D_{k-1}^{k}(\bullet)
is the image of
\pi_{k-1}^{k}
.

On the other hand, although
\pi_{k-1}^{k}:D^{k}(f)\rightarrow D^{k-1}(f)
has the problem that the target is an
icis
as well, the homology of
D_{k-1}^{k}(f_{t})
is well defined for any
\mathscr{A}
-finite
f:(\mathbb{C}^{n},S)\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{n+1},0)
of corank
1
. This is a consequence of
Theorem

3.7
applied to
\pi_{k-1}^{k}:D^{k}(f_{t})\rightarrow D^{k-1}(f_{t})
. Hence, again by the iteration principle, the Betti numbers of
D_{k-1}^{k}(f_{t})
are determined by the
\Sigma_{i+1}
-alternated homologies of
\left(D^{k+i}(F),D^{k+i}(f_{t})\right)
, for
i=0,\dots,d(f)-k
.

Furthermore,
D^{k+i}(f_{t})
is the unique fiber, up to isomorphism, of
D^{k+i}(f)
and the action of the permutations does not depend on the stable perturbation, so these Betti numbers will be well defined.

Finally, note that the homology will appear again in middle dimension for the pair of dimensions
(n,n+1)
, for the same reason it happens for
D(f_{t})
. Hence, we will simply write
\beta_{k}(f)
to denote
\beta_{n-k+1}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D_{k-1}^{k}(f_{t})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
.

We have compared
\beta_{1}(f)\coloneqq\mu_{I}(f)
with
\beta_{2}(f)=\mu_{D}(f)
and, similarly, we can compare
\beta_{k}(f)
with
\beta_{k+1}(f)
. This is very easy if
d(f)<n+1
or we have a mono-germ, because we can forget about the the homology of the pair and the unfolding by the exact sequence of the pair. As we were saying, by
Theorem

3.7
and the iteration principle, arranging the terms in a convenient way, we have

(5)

\beta_{k+1}(f)=\operatorname{rank}\bigoplus_{i\geq 2}H^{\textnormal{Alt}_{i}}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{k+i}(f_{t})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}\oplus H\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{k+1}(f_{t})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}

and

(6)

\beta_{k}(f)=\operatorname{rank}\bigoplus_{i\geq 1}H^{\textnormal{Alt}_{i+1}}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{k+i}(f_{t})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}\oplus H\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{k}(f_{t})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}},

from where we have omitted the index of the homology. Therefore, subtracting
Equation

6
from
Equation

5
and using the ideas of
Theorems

5.4
and
5.5
, we get

(7)

\begin{aligned} \beta_{k+1}(f)-\beta_{k}(f)=&\sum_{i\geq 2}\frac{\textnormal{rank }H\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{k+i}(f_{t})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}^{(2,1,\dots,1)}}{i}+\mu\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{k+1}(f)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}\\
&-\mu\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{k}(f)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}-\textnormal{rank }H^{\textnormal{Alt}_{2}}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{k+1}(f_{t})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}\end{aligned},

with
(2,1,\dots,1)
partition of
i+1
.

Remark
.

One can take this to a broader context as long as the first page of the spectral sequence collapses and the iteration principle works.

We put this in practice with some examples.

Example 5.9
.

For
f:(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{3},0)
as in
Theorem

5.4
and taking
k=1
in
Equation

7
,

\displaystyle\mu_{D}(f)-\mu_{I}(f)

\displaystyle=\frac{\textnormal{ rank }H_{0}\left(D^{3}(f_{t})\right)^{(2,1)}}{2}+\mu\left(D^{2}(f)\right)-0-\mu_{2}^{\textnormal{Alt}}(f)

\displaystyle=\frac{\textnormal{ rank }H_{0}\left(D^{3}(f_{t})\right)^{(2,1)}}{2}+\operatorname{rank}H_{1}\left(D^{2}(f_{t})\right)^{(2)},

where
H_{1}\big(D^{2}(f_{t})\big)^{(2)}
is the part of the homology that is fixed by the group
\Sigma_{2}
, i.e., the
trivial isotype
of
\Sigma_{2}
. The last equality is due to the fact that there are only two irreducible representations of
\Sigma_{2}
, the alternating and the
trivial
one.

Example 5.10
.

For
f:(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{3},0)
as in
Theorem

5.4
, and taking
k=2
in
Equation

7
,

\displaystyle\beta_{3}(f)-\mu_{D}(f)

\displaystyle=\textnormal{rank }H_{0}\left(D^{3}(f_{t})\right)-\mu\left(D^{2}(f)\right)-\textnormal{rank }H_{0}^{\textnormal{Alt}_{2}}\left(D^{3}(f_{t})\right)

\displaystyle=-\mu\left(D^{2}(f)\right)+\textnormal{rank }H_{0}\left(D^{3}(f_{t})\right)^{(2)},

following the same notation as above.

Note that the triple points of
f_{t}
are strict in
Examples

5.9
and
5.10
, i.e., in
D^{3}(f_{t})
the points are the
\Sigma_{3}
-orbit of
(a,b,c)
with
a\neq b\neq c\neq a
. Say we have
T
triple points, then

•

\textnormal{ rank }H_{0}\left(D^{3}(f_{t})\right)^{(2,1)}=4T
, as it is the complement of the alternating and trivial isotype and both representations have dimension one.

•

\textnormal{rank }H_{0}\left(D^{3}(f_{t})\right)^{(2)}=3T
, as it is the trivial isotype of
\Sigma_{2}
fixing the first entry (it has elements of the form
(a,b,c)+(a,c,b)
).

•

\operatorname{rank}H_{0}^{\textnormal{Alt}}\left(D^{3}(f_{t})\right)=3T
, similarly as the previous case.

•

\beta_{3}(f)=6T
, as it is simply counting the elements of the orbits.

In conclusion, we have the following results, which were obtained also by Houston with similar invariants (see
[
15
, Proofs of Theorems 2.7 and 2.8]
).

Theorem 5.11
.

For
f:(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{3},0)

\mathscr{A}
-finite and being
T
the number of triple points of a stable perturbation of
f
,

\mu_{D}(f)=\mu\left(D^{2}(f)\right)+3T.

Proof.

If
f
has corank 1, we are done by
Proposition

3.13
. If
f
does not have corank one,
D^{2}
has dimension one and
D^{3}
is zero dimensional, so the homology is in middle dimension and the same argument can be applied.

∎

Similarly:

Theorem 5.12
.

For
f:(\mathbb{C}^{3},0)\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{4},0)
as in
Theorem

5.4
and being
Q
the number of quadruple points of a stable perturbation
f_{t}
of
f
,

\mu_{D}(f)=4Q+\mu\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}(f)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}+\frac{\mu\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{3}(f)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}-\mu_{3}^{T}(f)+\mu_{3}^{\textnormal{Alt}}(f)}{2},

where
\mu_{3}^{T}(f)\coloneqq\textnormal{rank }H_{1}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{3}(f_{t})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}^{(3)}
is the invariant homology by
\Sigma_{3}
and
\mu_{3}^{\textnormal{Alt}}(f)
is defined as in
Definition

3.12
, i.e., the alternating homology by
\Sigma_{3}
.

Proof.

By
Equation

7
for
k=1
, we have that

\displaystyle\mu_{D}(f)-\mu_{I}(f)=

\displaystyle\frac{\textnormal{rank }H\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{3}(f_{t})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}^{(2,1)}}{2}+\frac{\textnormal{rank }H\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{4}(f_{t})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}^{(2,1,1)}}{3}

\displaystyle+\mu\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}(f)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}-0-\mu_{2}^{\textnormal{Alt}}(f).

Now, observe that
\Sigma_{3}
has only three irreducible representations: the trivial representation, alternating representation and the
(2,1)
-representation. With this in mind,

\frac{\textnormal{rank }H\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{3}(f_{t})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}^{(2,1)}}{2}=\frac{\textnormal{rank }H\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{3}(f_{t})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}-\mu_{3}^{T}(f)-\mu_{3}^{\textnormal{Alt}}(f)}{2}.

Finally, recall that
\mu_{I}(f)=\mu_{2}^{\textnormal{Alt}}(f)+\mu_{3}^{\textnormal{Alt}}(f)+\mu_{4}^{\textnormal{Alt}}(f)
(see
[
17
, Definition 3.11]
or
Proposition

3.13
). Furthermore,

\frac{\textnormal{rank }H\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{4}(f_{t})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}^{(2,1,1)}}{3}=3Q

and

\mu_{4}^{\textnormal{Alt}}(f)=Q.

The result follows from here.
∎

There is another relation between
\mu_{I}
and
\mu_{D}
.
Let
f:(\mathbb{C}^{n},S)\to(\mathbb{C}^{n+1},0)
be
\mathscr{A}
-finite of corank one and let
g:(\mathbb{C}^{n-1},S^{\prime})\to(\mathbb{C}^{n},0)
be a transverse slice. By the Lê-Greuel type formula (recall
Theorems

4.1
and
4.3
), we know that

\mu_{I}(f)+\mu_{I}(g)=\textnormal{{\#}}\Sigma(p|_{Z_{s}})=\sum_{\mathcal{Q}}\textnormal{{\#}}\Sigma\big(p|_{\mathcal{Q}(f_{s})}\big),

where
p:\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\to\mathbb{C}
is the generic projection which defines the transverse slice,
Z_{s}
is the image of a stable perturbation of
f
,
\mathcal{Q}
runs through all the stable types in the target and
\mathcal{Q}(f_{s})
denotes the points of
f_{s}
in the target that are of stable type
\mathcal{Q}
.

Using the same argument, we have that

\mu_{D}(f)+\mu_{D}(g)=\textnormal{{\#}}\Sigma(p\circ f_{s})=\sum_{\mathcal{Q}_{S}}\textnormal{{\#}}\Sigma\big((p\circ f_{s})|_{\mathcal{Q}_{S}(f_{s})}\big),

where now
\mathcal{Q}_{S}
runs through all the stable types in the source.

If a stable type in the source
\mathcal{Q}_{S}
corresponds to
\mathcal{Q}
in the target, the restriction
f_{s}:\mathcal{Q}_{S}(f_{s})\to\mathcal{Q}(f_{s})
is a local diffeomorphism and is
r
-to-one, where
r=r(\mathcal{Q})
is the number of branches of the stable type
\mathcal{Q}
. Hence,

\textnormal{{\#}}\Sigma((p\circ f_{s})|_{\mathcal{Q}_{S}(f_{s})})=r(\mathcal{Q})\textnormal{{\#}}\Sigma(p|_{\mathcal{Q}(f_{s})}).

Therefore, if
\mu_{I}(f_{t})
and
\mu_{I}(g_{t})
are constant in a family, this implies that
\mu_{D}(f_{t})
and
\mu_{D}(g_{t})
are also constant, by upper semi-continuity.

Proposition 5.13
.

Let
f:(\mathbb{C}^{n},S)\to(\mathbb{C}^{n+1},0)
be
\mathscr{A}
-finite of corank one and let
g:(\mathbb{C}^{n-1},S^{\prime})\to(\mathbb{C}^{n},0)
be a transverse slice. If
\mu_{I}(f_{t})
and
\mu_{I}(g_{t})
are constant in a family, then
\mu_{D}(f_{t})
and
\mu_{D}(g_{t})
are also constant.

The previous argument could make the reader think that, when we deal with Whitney equisingularity, controlling the target is enough to control the source, or vice versa. This idea is wrong in general. We take care of the details to control the target and the source in
Section

6
, and the problem with the previous idea is that
\mu_{D}(g)
is not what we need (see
Corollaries

6.4
and
6.7
). See also
Example

6.8
for an example where this idea fails.

6.
Whitney equisingularity

In
[
5
]
, Gaffney showed that a one parameter family
f_{t}:(\mathbb{C}^{n},S)\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{n+1},0)
is Whitney equisingular if, and only if, it is excellent (in Gaffney’s sense) and all the polar multiplicities in the source and target are constant on
t
. The problem is that, for each
d
-dimensional stratum in the source or target, we need
d+1
invariants, so the total number of invariants we need to control the Whitney equisingularity is huge.

In this section, we follow the approach of Teissier in
[
38
]
for hypersurfaces with isolated singularities or Gaffney in
[
6
]
for
icis
to show that, in the corank one case, Whitney equisingularity can be characterized in terms of the
\mu_{I}^{*}(f_{t})
and
\mu_{I}^{*}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}(f_{t}),\pi\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
sequences, obtained by taking successive transverse slices of
f_{t}
.

In
Section

4
, we already made use of the stratification
\mathscr{S}
by stable types of the image of a locally stable mapping
f:X\to Y
between smooth manifolds
X
and
Y
with
\operatorname{dim}X=n
and
\operatorname{dim}Y=n+1
and with only corank one singularities. Since each stable type is determined by its Mather algebra
\mathcal{Q}
, we can denote by
\mathcal{Q}(f)
the stratum of points
y\in f(X)
such that the multi-germ of
f
at
y
has type
\mathcal{Q}
.
Because
f
is stable,
\mathscr{S}
is a partial stratification of
f
in the sense of
[
7
, Proposition 3.1]
. It follows that we have an induced stratification
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
on
X
, with strata
\mathcal{Q}_{S}(f)=f^{-1}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}\mathcal{Q}(f)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
, such that

f:X\to Y
is a Thom stratified map.

Suppose, now, that we have an
\mathscr{A}
-finite germ
f:(X,S)\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{n+1},0)
of corank one, where
X
is an
n
-dimensional
icis
. By
Proposition

2.8
, we can take a finite representative
f:X\to Y
, where
Y
is an open neighbourhood of 0 in
\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
such that
f^{-1}(0)=S
and
f:X\setminus S\to Y\setminus\{0\}
is a locally stable mapping. The stratification by stable types on
f:X\setminus S\to Y\setminus\{0\}
extends to
f:X\to Y
just by adding
S
and
\{0\}
as strata in the source and target, respectively. By shrinking the representative if necessary, we can always assume that
f
has no
0
-stable singularities, so
S
and
\{0\}
are in fact the only 0-dimensional strata.

Finally, we give a version of excellency, stratification by stable types and Whitney equisingularity for unfoldings of germs on
icis
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\pi,F,j)
be a one parameter unfolding of
(X,f)
which is origin preserving (that is,
S\subset X_{t}
and
f_{t}(S)=0
, for all
t
) so we can see the unfolding as a family of germs
f_{t}:(X_{t},S)\to(\mathbb{C}^{n+1},0)
.

Definition 6.1
.

We say that
(\mathcal{X},\pi,F,j)
is
excellent
if there exist a representative
F:\mathcal{X}\to Y\times U
, where
Y
and
T
are open neighbourhoods of the origin in
\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
and
\mathbb{C}
respectively, such that for all
t\in T
,
f_{t}^{-1}(0)=S
and
f_{t}:X_{t}\setminus S\to Y\setminus\{0\}
is a locally stable mapping with no
0
-stable singularities.

When the unfolding is excellent,
F:\mathcal{X}\setminus S\times\{0\}\to(Y\setminus\{0\})\times T
is also stable, so we have a well defined stratification by stable types. This extends to
F:\mathcal{X}\to Y\times T
just by adding
S\times T
and
\{0\}\times T
as strata in the source and target, respectively. These are, in fact, the only 1-dimensional strata.

Definition 6.2
.

We say that
(\mathcal{X},\pi,F,j)
is
Whitney equisingular
if
F:\mathcal{X}\to Y\times T
is a Thom stratified map with the stratification by stable types.

Now, we recall the definition of polar multiplicities, following Gaffney in
[
5
]
.

Definition 6.3
.

Let
f:(X,S)\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{n+1},0)
be
\mathscr{A}
-finite of corank one. For each stable type
\mathcal{Q}
such that
d=\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{Q}(f)>0
and for each
i=0,\dots,d-1
, the
i
th-polar multiplicities
in the source and target are

m_{i}(f,\mathcal{Q})=m_{0}\left(P_{i}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}\overline{\mathcal{Q}(f)}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}\right),\quad m_{i}(f,\mathcal{Q}_{S})=m_{0}\left(P_{i}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}\overline{\mathcal{Q}_{S}(f)}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}\right),

where the bar means the Zariski closure and
P_{i}(Z)
is the absolute polar variety of codimension
i
of
Z
in the sense of Lê and Teissier in
[
42
, p. 462]
.

The
d
th-stable multiplicities
are

\displaystyle m_{d}(f,\mathcal{Q})

\displaystyle=\deg\left(\pi:P_{d}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}\overline{\mathcal{Q}(F)},\pi\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}\to(\mathbb{C}^{r},0)\right),

\displaystyle m_{d}(f,\mathcal{Q}_{S})

\displaystyle=\deg\left(\pi:P_{d}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}\overline{\mathcal{Q}_{S}(F)},\pi\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}\to(\mathbb{C}^{r},0)\right),

where, now,
(\mathcal{X},\pi,F,j)
is an
r
-parameter versal unfolding of
(X,f)
and
P_{d}(\mathcal{Z},\pi)
is the relative polar variety of codimension
d
of a family
\pi:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{C}^{r}
(see
[
38
, Section IV.1]
).

Finally, we denote by
c(f)
the number of all
0
-stable singularities that appear in a stable perturbation of
(X,f)
.

It follows from the definition of relative polar variety that the top polar multiplicity
m_{d}(f,\mathcal{Q})
is equal to the number of critical points of
p|_{\mathcal{Q}(f_{s})}
, where
p:\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\to\mathbb{C}
is a generic linear projection and
(X_{s},f_{s})
is a stable perturbation of
(X,f)
. Since the
0
-stable singularities are also critical points of
p
in the stratified sense, we get the following reformulation of
Theorem

4.1
:

Corollary 6.4
.

For a corank
1
and
\mathscr{A}
-finite multi-germ
f:(X,S)\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{n+1},0)
,
X
an
icis
of dimension
\operatorname{dim}X=n>1
, let
p:\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a generic linear projection which defines a transverse slice
g:(Y,S)\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{n},0)
, where
Y=X\cap\left(p\circ f\right)^{-1}\left(0\right)
. Then,

\mu_{I}(X,f)+\mu_{I}(Y,g)=\sum_{\textnormal{dim}\mathcal{Q}(f_{s})=d>0}m_{d}(f,\mathcal{Q})+c(f),

where the sum runs on all
\mathcal{Q}
such that
\textnormal{dim}\mathcal{Q}(f_{s})=d
and all
d>0
.

Now, we define the
\mu_{I}^{*}
and
\mu_{D}^{*}
-sequences of a corank one map germ.

Definition 6.5
.

Consider
f:(X,S)\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{n+1},0)

\mathscr{A}
-finite of corank one, with
X
an
icis
of dimension
\operatorname{dim}X=n>1
. We take a generic flag of vector subspaces

H_{(n-1)}\subset\dots\subset H_{(1)}\subset H_{(0)}=\mathbb{C}^{n+1},

such that
H_{(i)}
has codimension
i
. We put
X_{(i)}=X\cap f^{-1}\left(H_{(i)}\right)
and
f_{(i)}=\left.f\right|_{X_{(i)}}

and define the
\mu_{I}^{*}
-sequence of
(X,f)
as

\mu_{I}^{*}(X,f)\coloneqq\left(\mu_{I}(X,f),\mu_{I}\left(X_{(1)},f_{(1)},\right),\dots,\mu_{I}\left(X_{(n-1)},f_{(n-1)}\right)\right).

Sometimes we do not consider the top image Milnor number
\mu_{I}(X,f)
in the
\mu_{I}^{*}
-sequence and, then, we denote it by
\tilde{\mu}_{I}^{*}(X,f)
.

It is well-known that, by generic, we mean a suitable Zariski open in a convenient space, and this definition does not depend on the generic flag we are taking. The details can be seen in
[
23
, pp. 1380–1381]
.

In next lemma, we see that all polar multiplicities can be seen as top polar multiplicities of the corresponding transverse slices.

Lemma 6.6
.

With the hypothesis and notation of
Definition

6.5
, suppose that
\mathcal{Q}
is a stable type such that
\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{Q}(f_{s})=d>0
, for a stable perturbation
(X_{s},f_{s})
of
(X,f)
. Then,

m_{d-i}\left(f_{(i)},\mathcal{Q}\right)=m_{d-i}\left(f,\mathcal{Q}\right),\ i=1,\dots,d.

Proof.

By induction, it is enough to prove that

m_{d-i}\left(f_{(1)},\mathcal{Q}\right)=m_{d-i}\left(f,\mathcal{Q}\right),\ i=1,\dots,d.

To see this, we first observe that
\mathcal{Q}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}f_{(1)}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}=\mathcal{Q}(f)\cap H_{(1)}
, so the equality for
i=2,\dots,d
follows directly from
[
42
, Corollary 4.1.9]
.

For
i=1
, we can see
f
as a stabilisation of
f_{(1)}
.
If
\ell:\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\to\mathbb{C}
is the linear form such that
H_{(1)}=\ell^{-1}(0)
, this means that
f|_{\ell^{-1}(t)}
, with
t\neq 0
, is a stable perturbation of
f_{(1)}
.
In particular,
m_{d-1}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}f_{(1)},\mathcal{Q}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
is the number of critical points of a generic linear projection
p:\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\to\mathbb{C}
restricted to
\mathcal{Q}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}f|_{\ell^{-1}(t)}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}=\mathcal{Q}(f)\cap\ell^{-1}(t)
. This number can be also seen as

(8)

\deg\left(\ell:P_{d-1}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}\overline{\mathcal{Q}(f)},\ell\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}\to(\mathbb{C},0)\right),

where
P_{d-1}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}\overline{\mathcal{Q}(f)},\ell\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
is the closure of the set of critical points of
(p,\ell)|_{\mathcal{Q}(f)}
.

On the other hand,
m_{d-i}\left(f,\mathcal{Q}\right)=m_{0}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}P_{d-1}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}\overline{\mathcal{Q}(f)}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
. Since
P_{d-1}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}\overline{\mathcal{Q}(f)}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
is 1-dimensional and
\ell
is generic, this is equal to

(9)

\deg\left(\ell:P_{d-1}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}\overline{\mathcal{Q}(f)}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}\to(\mathbb{C},0)\right),

where
P_{d-1}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}\overline{\mathcal{Q}(f)}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
is again the closure of the set of critical points of
(p,\ell)|_{\mathcal{Q}(f)}
. So,
Equations

8
and
9
are equal.
∎

We arrive to the main theorem, which characterises the Whitney equisingularity of a family of map germs in terms of the
\mu_{I}^{*}
-sequences of
f_{t}
and
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}(f_{t}),\pi\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
.

Theorem 6.7
.

Let
f_{t}\colon(\mathbb{C}^{n},S)\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{n+1},0)
be a one parameter family of
\mathscr{A}
-finite corank one map germs. Then, the family is Whitney equisingular if, and only if, the sequences
\mu_{I}^{*}(f_{t})
and
\tilde{\mu}_{I}^{*}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}(f_{t}),\pi\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
are constant on
t
.

Proof.

Suppose that the sequences
\mu_{I}^{*}(f_{t})
and
\tilde{\mu}_{I}^{*}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}(f_{t}),\pi\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
are constant.
First of all, the constancy of
\mu_{I}(f_{t})
implies that the family is excellent (see
[
8
, Theorem 4.3]
).
In fact, this holds not only for the family
f_{t}
, but also for all families
{f_{t}}_{(i)}
,
i=1,\dots,n-1
. In particular, all the numbers of 0-stable singularities
c\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}{f_{t}}_{(i)}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
are constant (see
[
5
, Proposition 3.6]
).
By Gaffney’s results in
[
5
, Theorems 7.1 and 7.3]
, we need to proof that all polar invariants in the source and target are constant.

By
Lemma

6.6
, the constancy of the polar multiplicities follows from the constancy of the top polar multiplicities of all the transverse slices
{f_{t}}_{(i)}
, with
i=1,\dots,n-1
. Secondly, we apply recursively
Corollary

6.4
on
{f_{t}}_{(i)}
for
i=0,\dots,n-2
. For any
i
, we have

\mu_{I}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}{f_{t}}_{(i)}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}+\mu_{I}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}{f_{t}}_{(i+1)}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}=\sum_{\textnormal{dim}\mathcal{Q}({f_{t}}_{(i)})=d>0}m_{d}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}{f_{t}}_{(i)},\mathcal{Q}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}+c\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}{f_{t}}_{\left(i\right)}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}.

The polar multiplicities
m_{d}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}{f_{t}}_{\left(i\right)},\mathcal{Q}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
are upper semi-continuous (see
[
5
, Proposition 4.15]
). Therefore, all
m_{d}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}{f_{t}}_{(i)},\mathcal{Q}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
must be constant.

For the polar multiplicities in the source, we follow the same argument, but this time applied to the family
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}(f_{t}),\pi\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
. Observe that the polar multiplicities of
{f_{t}}_{(i)}
in the source coincide with the polar multiplicities of
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}({f_{t}}_{(i)}),\pi\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
in the target. Hence, we need to study
\mu_{I}^{*}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}({f_{t}}_{(i)}),\pi\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
.
Moreover, it follows from
Proposition

5.13
that

\mu_{D}(f_{t})+\mu_{D}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}{f_{t}}_{(1)}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}=\sum_{\mathcal{Q}_{S}}r(\mathcal{Q})\textnormal{{\#}}\Sigma\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}p|_{\mathcal{Q}(f_{s})}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}.

Since the right-hand side is constant, for all the members in the sum are either polar multiplicities in the target or numbers of
0
-stable invariants, so is the left-hand side. Again, the upper semi-continuity of
\mu_{D}
(see
Corollary

2.15
) implies that
\mu_{D}(f_{t})
is also constant. Hence, it is enough to consider the reduced sequence
\tilde{\mu}^{*}_{I}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}(f_{t}),\pi\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
, as
\mu_{D}(f_{t})=\mu_{I}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}(f_{t}),\pi\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
.

Finally, the converse is easy. If the family
f_{t}
is Whitney equisingular, so are the families
{f_{t}}_{(i)}
, for
i=1,\dots,n-1
. By Thom’s second isotopy lemma, they are topologically trivial and, hence, their image Milnor numbers are constant (see
[
8
, Corollary 2.10]
). Analogously, the family
\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}(f_{t}),\pi\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
must be also Whitney equisingular, which gives the constancy of the sequence
\tilde{\mu}^{*}_{I}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}(f_{t}),\pi\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
.
∎

The following example shows that
\tilde{\mu}^{*}_{I}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}(f_{t}),\pi\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
, or even
\mu^{*}_{I}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}(f_{t}),\pi\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
, is not enough to control Whitney equisingularity.

Example 6.8
.

Consider the one parameter family
f_{t}:(\mathbb{C}^{2},0)\to(\mathbb{C}^{3},0)
such that

f_{t}(x,y)=(x,y^{4},x^{5}y-5x^{3}y^{3}+4xy^{5}+y^{6}+ty^{7}).

This family is an example of topologically trivial family such that it is not Whitney equisingular, which was shown in
[
35
, Example 5.5]
.

In this example, we also have constancy of
\mu\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D(f_{t})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
. Therefore, the source is Whitney regular as
D(f_{t})
is a family of plane curves. In particular, all the multiplicities in the source and
\mu_{I}^{*}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D^{2}(f_{t}),\pi\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
are constant. However, neither the polar multiplicities in the target nor
\mu_{I}^{*}(f_{t})
are constant because the family is not Whitney equisingular (and by
Theorem

6.7
).

The proof of
Theorem

6.7
allows us to state a partial result when only the sequence
\mu_{I}^{*}(f_{t})
is constant.

Definition 6.9
.

We say that the family
f_{t}
is
Whitney equisingular in the target
if there exists a representative of the unfolding
F:\mathcal{X}\to Y\times T
as in
Definition

6.2
such that the stratification by stable types on
Y\times T
is a Whitney stratification.

Proposition 6.10
.

Let
f_{t}\colon(\mathbb{C}^{n},S)\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{n+1},0)
be a one-parameter family of
\mathscr{A}
-finite corank one map germs. Then, the family is Whitney equisingular in the target if, and only if, the sequence
\mu_{I}^{*}(f_{t})
is constant on
t
.

Example 6.11
.

Related to
Example

6.8
, there are families
f_{t}
that are Whitney equisingular in the target, but they are not Whitney equisingular. In
[
NunoBallesteros2022
]
, Tomazella, Silva, and the second author prove that the family
f_{t}:(\mathbb{C}^{3},0)\to(\mathbb{C}^{4},0)
such that

f_{t}(x,y,z)=(x,y,z^{5}+xz,z^{7}+yz+tz^{8})

has

\displaystyle\mu_{I}^{*}(f_{t})

\displaystyle=(25,16,10),\forall t,

\displaystyle\tilde{\mu}_{I}\big(D^{2}(f_{0}),\pi\big)

\displaystyle=(65,9),\text{ for }t=0,

\displaystyle\ \tilde{\mu}_{I}\big(D^{2}(f_{t}),\pi\big)

\displaystyle=(65,8),\text{ for }t\neq 0.

This shows that the family is Whitney equisingular only in the target (by
Theorems

6.7
and
6.10
). This and
Example

6.8
enhance the importance of all the invariants of
Theorem

6.7
.

As a corollary of
Theorem

6.7
, and closing the topic we have started with
Example

6.8
, the Whitney equisingularity of a family
f_{t}:(\mathbb{C}^{2},S)\to(\mathbb{C}^{3},0)
is controlled by just two invariants in the target. In fact, in
[
23
]
it was shown that
f_{t}
(of any corank) is Whitney equisingular if, and only if,
\mu\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}D(f_{t})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
and
\mu\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}\operatorname{Im}({f_{t}}_{(1)})\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
are constant, where
\mu
is the usual Milnor number of a plane curve.

Corollary 6.12
.

Let
f_{t}\colon(\mathbb{C}^{2},S)\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{3},0)
be a one parameter family of
\mathscr{A}
-finite corank one map germs. Then, the family is Whitney equisingular if, and only if,
\mu_{I}(f_{t})
and
\mu_{I}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle($}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle($}}}}}{f_{t}}_{(1)}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\displaystyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.5pt}{$\textstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.75pt}{$\scriptstyle)$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.25pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle)$}}}}}
are constant on
t
.
